    "newString": "Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) drops from 5/12 to 0--1/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) remains consistently high (7--8/12). Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions (positive in 10, negative in 2 position-bias conditions). $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with the weakest-link monotonicity property (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}). Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 8/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12). Crossed random-effects analysis confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance at $K{\\geq}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 5/12  & 8/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 8/12  & 10/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); positive direction is preserved in 10/12 conditions across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}); positive coefficient direction (10/12) is preserved across all methods.\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$, $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$, $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12), suggesting diverse judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses (Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast}).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in 10 of 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\paragraph{Position Bias Exception.}\nPosition bias stands apart: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both MMLU and ARC ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible to position bias. Position bias is a shared vulnerability---all judges exhibit the same directional preference---so independent judges provide genuine redundancy against this correlated failure mode, consistent with the Condorcet intuition. The two regimes (10 positive, 2 negative) thus align with the targeted-vs-shared attack taxonomy: targeted attacks exploit weak links (positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$), while shared-bias attacks are diluted by diversity (negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 9/12  & 8+/1$-$   & 0.745 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.349 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.336 \\\\\nBayesian        & 8/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.136 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.745$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.136$), implying panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 10--11/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 8/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12). Crossed random-effects analysis confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance at $K{\\geq}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 5/12  & 8/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 8/12  & 10/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); positive direction is preserved in 10/12 conditions across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}); positive coefficient direction (10/12) is preserved across all methods.\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$, $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$, $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12), suggesting diverse judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses (Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}).\n\n\\subsection{Positive Direction Across Attack Types}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction in 10 of 12 conditions (88.9--100\\% positive rate; Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}), with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Kruskal--Wallis $p{=}0.200$, $0.423$).\n\n\\paragraph{Position Bias Exception.}\nPosition bias stands apart: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both MMLU and ARC ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible to position bias. Position bias is a shared vulnerability---all judges exhibit the same directional preference---so independent judges provide genuine redundancy against this correlated failure mode, consistent with the Condorcet intuition. The two regimes (10 positive, 2 negative) thus align with the targeted-vs-shared attack taxonomy: targeted attacks exploit weak links (positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$), while shared-bias attacks are diluted by diversity (negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$).\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 9/12  & 8+/1$-$   & 0.745 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.349 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.336 \\\\\nBayesian        & 8/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.136 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.745$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.136$), implying panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 10--11/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

290	
291	\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}
292	\label{app:bft_reversal}
293	
294	Under standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\eta_{\max}$ significant in 10 of 12 conditions and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in all 12. Non-independence correction attenuates both predictors: under wild cluster bootstrap, $\eta_{\max}$ retains 5/12 and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains 8/12. The directional pattern ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positive in 10/12 conditions) persists across methods. The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship whose statistical confidence attenuates under adaptive attack (8/12 after correction).
295	
296	\begin{figure}[h]
297	\centering
298	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
299	\caption{GAM bootstrap $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction at $K{=}3$ ($B{=}9999$). 10 of 12 conditions show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction (88.9--100\% positive rate); both position-bias conditions show negative direction. The Kruskal--Wallis test finds no significant magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks ($p{=}0.200$).}
300	\label{fig:gam_split}
301	\end{figure}
302	
303	Under corrected data (V3, with position-bias fix), 10 of 12 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ OLS coefficients are positive; the two position-bias conditions (MMLU, ARC) show negative coefficients ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$ at $K{=}3$). GAM bootstrap confirms positive direction in 10/12 conditions at $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$.
304	
305	\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}
306	\label{app:diversity_capability}
307	
308	The $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
309	
```

Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions (positive in 10, negative in 2 position-bias conditions). $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with the weakest-link monotonicity property (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$, $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$, $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The two exceptions are position bias: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both benchmarks ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible. Because position bias is a shared vulnerability where all judges exhibit the same directional preference, independent judges provide genuine redundancy---consistent with the Condorcet intuition and the targeted-vs-shared distinction in the attack taxonomy.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 9/12  & 8+/1$-$   & 0.745 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.349 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.336 \\\\\nBayesian        & 8/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.136 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 10--11/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

  "old_string": "\\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) prevents observational regression from reliably separating their contributions; capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\\textbf{Inference and measurement sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is method-sensitive (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 8/12, fractional logit 10/12), though direction is positive in 10 of 12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; the diversity significance trajectory is also operationalization-dependent ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ $\\kappa$-diversity show opposite $K$-trends).\n\\textbf{Benchmark and attack scope.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation may present different attack surfaces. The six local models use AWQ INT4 quantization, which may confound capability classification, though excluding near-chance quantized models preserves the association (OLS 10--12/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:near_chance}). Each attack uses 1--2 template variants, limiting generalization. We study only odd $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$; the predictor shift threshold $K^*$ is not precisely identified.\n\\textbf{Aggregation scope.} We examine only majority vote, threshold, and Bayesian calibration as aggregation rules; more sophisticated strategies (e.g., weighted voting, learned aggregation, or iterative deliberation among judges) may alter the diversity--robustness relationship and are not explored here.\n\\textbf{Temporal snapshot.} All API model evaluations were collected during a fixed time window; providers may update model weights or safety filters without notice, potentially altering individual susceptibility profiles and panel-level robustness patterns.",

  "toolUseResult": {

    "oldString": "\\title{The Diminishing Weakest Link: How Panel Size Shifts\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability Drivers in LLM Judge Panels}",
    "newString": "\\title{The Diminishing Weakest Link: Panel Size and Predictor\\\\Relevance in Multi-Judge LLM Evaluation under Adversarial Manipulation}",
    "originalFile": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{inconsolata}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{amsmath,amssymb,amsthm}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\\usepackage{subcaption}\n\\usepackage{tcolorbox}\n\\tcbuselibrary{breakable}\n\n\\newtheorem{theorem}{Theorem}\n\\newtheorem{definition}{Definition}\n\\newtheorem{proposition}{Proposition}\n\n\\title{The Diminishing Weakest Link: How Panel Size Shifts\\\\Adversarial Vulnerability Drivers in LLM Judge Panels}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work disentangles whether robustness gains stem from individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity. We decompose panel attack success rate into weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$) and effective independence ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two multiple-choice benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is positively associated with vulnerability ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ positive in 10/12 conditions, $\\kappa$-diversity 12/12), and (2)~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.243 \\to 0.091 \\to 0.086$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/cert_manip_resist_eval-588E/}.}.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) prevents observational regression from reliably separating their contributions; capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\\textbf{Inference and measurement sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is method-sensitive (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 8/12, fractional logit 10/12), though direction is positive in 10/12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; the diversity significance trajectory is operationalization-dependent ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ $\\kappa$-diversity show opposite $K$-trends).\n\\textbf{Scope and confounds.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation may present different attack surfaces. The six local AWQ INT4 models may confound capability classification, though excluding near-chance models preserves the association (Appendix~\\ref{app:near_chance}). Each attack uses 1--2 template variants, limiting generalization. All API evaluations were collected during a fixed time window (May 2026); provider updates may alter susceptibility profiles.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\end{document}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Experimental Methodology}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Models and Panel Construction}\n\nWe select 15 LLM judges spanning 6 provider families and 3 capability tiers, listed in Table~\\ref{tab:models}. Nine models are accessed via the OpenRouter API;\\footnote{All API models were accessed via OpenRouter between March--April 2026. Model snapshots may have changed after this period.} six open-weight models are served locally using vLLM~0.21.0 with Activation-aware Weight Quantization (AWQ) INT4 on an NVIDIA RTX PRO~6000 Blackwell (96\\,GB). API models are assigned to \\emph{frontier}, \\emph{mid-tier}, or \\emph{lightweight} tiers following the provider capability classification; all six local models fall into the lightweight tier, as INT4 quantization substantially reduces judge accuracy on the pairwise judgment task (Table~\\ref{tab:models}). We study three panel sizes $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ (all odd, majority-vote threshold $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$). Exhaustive enumeration yields $\\binom{15}{3} = 455$ panels at $K{=}3$, $\\binom{15}{5} = 3{,}003$ at $K{=}5$, and $\\binom{15}{7} = 6{,}435$ at $K{=}7$, covering every combination of families and tiers. Each panel evaluates all benchmark items under all attack types, producing a complete panel $\\times$ item $\\times$ attack tensor. The primary analysis uses $K{=}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k} extends to larger panels.\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks and Attack Types}\n\nTwo benchmarks test distinct evaluation capabilities: MMLU \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}, a knowledge-intensive multiple-choice benchmark spanning 57 subjects, and ARC \\citep{clark2018arc}, a reasoning-intensive science question benchmark. We sample 300 items from each. All models use temperature~0 for deterministic outputs (temperature has negligible effect on $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$; Appendix~\\ref{app:temperature}).\n\nEach benchmark is paired with six attack types spanning targeted and shared-bias categories (Table~\\ref{tab:attack_taxonomy}): prompt injection \\citep{perez2022ignore,greshake2023not}, sycophancy \\citep{wang2023fair}, score manipulation, verbosity bias \\citep{zheng2023judging}, position bias \\citep{wang2023fair}, and authority bias. The 2 benchmarks $\\times$ 6 attacks produce 12 conditions, each evaluated across all 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:attacks}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Vulnerability Metrics}\n\nPer-judge vulnerability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a {=} 1)$ is estimated empirically; $\\eta_{\\max}(a) = \\max_{j \\in \\mathcal{P}} \\eta_j(a)$ captures the weakest link. Panel diversity $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (Equation~\\ref{eq:keff}) is computed from pairwise MI on clean winner labels using the plug-in estimator with Miller--Madow correction (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_derivation}). Under a Gaussian copula, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} = K - \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i}\\sum_{j \\neq i} \\rho_{ij}^2$ \\citep{nelsen2006copulas}; goodness-of-fit rejects the copula for 97--100\\% of pairs, yet rankings agree closely ($\\rho{\\approx}0.86$; Appendix~\\ref{app:copula_gof}). We also evaluate $\\kappa$-diversity \\citep{kuncheva2003measures}. The $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation is $r{=}0.70$ at $K{=}3$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter_main}), reflecting the structural coupling between diversity and capability in the current LLM ecosystem.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Methods}\n\nBeyond majority vote ($\\geq \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ approvals), we evaluate three alternatives: \\emph{accuracy-weighted vote} ($w_j \\propto \\mathrm{acc}_j$; accept if $\\sum_j w_j \\cdot \\mathbf{1}[J_j \\text{ approves}] > 0.5$), \\emph{threshold vote} (unanimity, $t{=}K$), and \\emph{Bayesian calibration} ($P(\\text{correct} \\mid \\mathbf{v}) \\propto P(\\text{correct}) \\prod_j P(v_j \\mid \\text{correct})$; accept if posterior $> 0.5$).\n\n\\subsection{Statistical Framework}\n\\label{sec:stat_framework}\n\nTo disentangle the contributions of panel diversity and individual judge robustness, we fit a log-log OLS regression for each of the 12 conditions:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:regression}\n\\log(\\mathrm{ASR} + \\epsilon) = \\alpha + \\beta_1 \\log(K_{\\mathrm{eff}}) + \\beta_2 \\log(\\eta_{\\max} + \\epsilon),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $\\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = \\hat{\\beta} \\cdot \\mathrm{SD}(\\log x) / \\mathrm{SD}(\\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) correction \\citep{benjamini1995controlling} for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate (FDR) per predictor across the 12 conditions.\n\nDominance analysis \\citep{budescu1993dominance} decomposes $R^2$ into each predictor's incremental contribution. GAMs with smooth terms test whether $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect varies between targeted and shared-bias attacks.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}\n\\label{sec:robustness_method}\n\nThe 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We apply three corrections: clustered SE (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models, and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, max-$p$) following \\citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. Fractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE serves as an $\\epsilon$-free robustness check. Monte Carlo calibration confirms the bootstrap does not over-reject (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:monte_carlo}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BkuNH6CqeHRYffQeTMTQSa`</summary>

```text
33	To quantify diversity, we define the \emph{effective independence} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information (MI) among judges:
34	\begin{equation}
35	\label{eq:keff}
36	K_{\mathrm{eff}} = \frac{1}{K}\sum_{i=1}^{K} \Bigl[1 + \sum_{j \neq i} \exp\!\bigl(-2 \cdot \mathrm{MI}(i,j)\bigr)\Bigr].
37	\end{equation}
38	This formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\mathrm{MI} \to 0$) to $K_{\mathrm{eff}} \approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\mathrm{MI} \to \infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\eta_{\mathrm{joint}} \sim K_{\mathrm{eff}} \cdot \eta_{\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.
39	
40	\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]
41	\label{def:adversary}
42	A \emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\{\eta_j(\cdot)\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \arg\max_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\mathrm{ASR}^*(\mathcal{P}) = \sup_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \mathrm{ASR}(\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.
43	\end{definition}
44	
45	This departs from BFT models~\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.
46	
47	\paragraph{Weakest-Link Monotonicity.}
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor shift across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) drops from 5/12 to 0--1/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) remains consistently high (7--8/12). Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions (positive in 10, negative in 2 position-bias conditions). $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with the weakest-link monotonicity property (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}). Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 8/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12). Crossed random-effects analysis confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance at $K{\\geq}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 5/12  & 8/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 8/12  & 10/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); positive direction is preserved in 10/12 conditions across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}); positive coefficient direction (10/12) is preserved across all methods.\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$, $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$, $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12), suggesting diverse judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses (Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}).\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Heterogeneity}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association in 10/12 conditions with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). The two exceptions are position bias: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both benchmarks ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible. Because position bias is a shared vulnerability where all judges exhibit the same directional preference, independent judges provide genuine redundancy---consistent with the Condorcet intuition and the targeted-vs-shared distinction in the attack taxonomy.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 9/12  & 8+/1$-$   & 0.745 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.349 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.336 \\\\\nBayesian        & 8/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.136 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Table~\\ref{tab:dominance} uses per-condition FDR; this table uses pooled FDR across conditions.}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nPredictability ranges from threshold ($R^2{=}0.745$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.136$), implying panel composition is almost unpredictive under calibrated aggregation. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 10--11/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor relevance across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) drops from 5/12 to 0--1/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) remains consistently high (8--8--7/12). Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions (positive in 10, negative in 2 position-bias conditions). $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with the weakest-link monotonicity property (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}). Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 8/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12). Crossed random-effects analysis confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance at $K{\\geq}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 5/12  & 8/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 8/12  & 10/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); positive direction is preserved in 10/12 conditions across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}); positive coefficient direction (10/12) is preserved across all methods.\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s dominance is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$, $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$, $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12), suggesting diverse judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses (Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}).\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Heterogeneity}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association in 10/12 conditions with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). The two exceptions are position bias: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both benchmarks ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible. Because position bias is a shared vulnerability where all judges exhibit the same directional preference, independent judges provide genuine redundancy---consistent with the Condorcet intuition and the targeted-vs-shared distinction in the attack taxonomy.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 9/12  & 8+/1$-$   & 0.745 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.349 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.336 \\\\\nBayesian        & 8/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.136 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12). At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 10--11/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictor relevance across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) drops from 5/12 to 0--1/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (blue) remains consistently high (8--8--7/12). Shaded region: $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher, max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\nUnder OLS, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is FDR-significant in all 12/12 conditions (positive in 10, negative in 2 position-bias conditions). $\\eta_{\\max}$ is significant in 10/12 (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with the weakest-link monotonicity property (\\S\\ref{sec:threat_model}). Dominance analysis yields 10 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and zero $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant conditions. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels (Appendix~\\ref{app:keff_dist}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: both predictors attenuate under bootstrap ($\\eta_{\\max}$ 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 8/12), with clustered SE widest ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 0/12). Crossed random-effects analysis confirms $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance at $K{\\geq}5$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:crossed_re}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ \\textbf{sig} & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ \\textbf{sig} \\\\\n\\midrule\nOLS                    & 10/12 & 12/12 \\\\\nClustered SE           & 4/12  & 0/12  \\\\\nMixed effects          & 7/12  & 3/12  \\\\\nWild bootstrap         & 5/12  & 8/12  \\\\\nFrac.\\ logit (clust.)  & 8/12  & 10/12 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Inference method sensitivity at $K{=}3$ (455 panels, 12 conditions). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance ranges from 0/12 (clustered SE) to 12/12 (OLS); positive direction is preserved in 10/12 conditions across all methods.}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\end{table}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}); positive coefficient direction (10/12) is preserved across all methods.\n\n\\subsection{The Diminishing Weakest Link Across Panel Sizes}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ pattern is method-sensitive (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), contrasting with $K{\\geq}5$ where $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s association is consistent (bootstrap 8/12; fractional logit 11/12). We repeat the bootstrap at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$ with max-$p$ correction.\n\nMeta-analytic pooling \\citep{dersimonian1986meta} quantifies trajectories independent of significance thresholds. $\\eta_{\\max}$'s pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ declines monotonically: $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$, $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$, $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero; Appendix~\\ref{app:meta_analytic_details}). Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} shows significance counts; per-condition effect sizes with 95\\% CIs are in Figure~\\ref{fig:forest_plot} (Appendix~\\ref{app:effect_size_forest}). The $\\eta_{\\max}$ decline holds under both $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\\kappa$-diversity.\n\n\nRandom faults yield $R^2{=}0.730$; adaptive attack reduces predictability ($R^2{=}0.285$ at $K{=}5$) but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains significance (7/12), suggesting diverse judges present distinct attack surfaces rather than redundant defenses (Appendix~\\ref{app:rf_contrast_detail}).\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Heterogeneity}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM bootstrap confirms positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ association in 10/12 conditions with no magnitude difference between targeted and shared-bias attacks (Appendix~\\ref{app:bft_reversal}). The two exceptions are position bias: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is negative in both benchmarks ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), indicating that diverse panels are \\emph{less} susceptible. Because position bias is a shared vulnerability where all judges exhibit the same directional preference, independent judges provide genuine redundancy---consistent with the Condorcet intuition and the targeted-vs-shared distinction in the attack taxonomy.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules and the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 9/12  & 8+/1$-$   & 0.745 \\\\\nMajority        & 10/12 & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.349 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 12/12 & 10+/2$-$  & 0.336 \\\\\nBayesian        & 8/12  & 6/12  & 5+/1$-$   & 0.136 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, OLS log-log). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. At $K{=}5$ all methods converge (7/12; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k5}); at $K{=}7$ Bayesian neutralization returns (Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_k7}).\n\n\\paragraph{Fractional Logit Robustness.}\nFractional logit \\citep{papke1996econometric} with clustered SE shows consistent positive association: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance is 10--11/12 across all panel sizes ($K{=}3,5,7$), with 98.6\\% directional concordance with OLS (71/72; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness_checks}).\n",

_source line 1670 · 2026-05-25T23:12:13.297Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019eKobxC7RtjrK4t5WR7e3Q`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
5	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ correlate at $r{=}0.70$--$0.81$ (VIF${\approx}2.0$--$2.9$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, Appendix~\ref{app:extended_analysis}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the confound could account for the association in 69\% of conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), though fractional logit confirms the positive association (10--11/12 across all $K$). Causal attribution requires capability-controlled designs (Appendices~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA},~\ref{app:cross_benchmark}).
6	
7	\paragraph{API-Only vs.\ Full Pool.}
8	Restricting to 9 API models removes the AWQ confound but increases collinearity ($r{=}0.85$): $\eta_{\max}$ retains significance (8/12) while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ drops (2--3/12); the API-only pool lacks statistical power (MDE $> 0.9$; Appendices~\ref{app:quant_dummy},~\ref{app:random_subsample}).
9	
10	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
11	Aggregation rules influence predictability but not $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction (Figure~\ref{fig:aggregation_heatmap}). Threshold voting yields the highest $R^2$ ($0.745$ at $K{=}3$), while Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{\leq}0.17$ across all $K$). At $K{=}7$, Bayesian calibration fully neutralizes $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (0/12; Appendix~\ref{app:bayesian_entropy}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in all 12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}), confirming that selecting judges by individual robustness dominates diversity-maximizing strategies regardless of panel size. Clean $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is stable under 4/6 attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}), offering a deployable metric from clean-condition agreement alone.
12	
13	\paragraph{Metric Sensitivity of the Diversity Trajectory.}
14	The diversity significance trajectory is operationalization-dependent: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ remains consistently high ($8 \to 8 \to 7$/12) while $\kappa$-diversity \citep{kuncheva2003measures} decreases ($8 \to 7 \to 6$/12; Appendix~\ref{app:kappa_regime}). Shannon MI entropy shows a third pattern ($4 \to 4 \to 0$; Appendix~\ref{app:shannon_entropy}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ confirms positive direction in 10/12 conditions, $\kappa$-diversity in 12/12; the $K$-dependent trajectory is metric-specific. The $\eta_{\max}$ decline holds under all specifications, making it the more robust finding across operationalizations.
15	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WQZ8UqrwR55p15J8b66zAa`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=0.78\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \textbf{(a)}~In small panels ($K{=}3$), a single vulnerable judge (red) can flip the majority; individual weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) dominates vulnerability ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$). \textbf{(b)}~As panel size grows, the balance shifts from individual weakness to collective diversity. \textbf{(c)}~In large panels ($K{\geq}5$), inter-judge diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}{=}0.353$) drives vulnerability while $\eta_{\max}$ diminishes (CI crosses zero). Bottom bars show relative predictive weight.}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.
11	
12	The conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.
13	
14	We address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}): weakest-link susceptibility ($\eta_{\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks (Massive Multitask Language Understanding [MMLU] and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge [ARC]) reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.
15	
16	These findings yield three contributions:
17	\begin{enumerate}
18	\item \textbf{Diversity positively associated with vulnerability.} $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ confirms a positive association with panel attack success in 10 of 12 conditions (10/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap); $\kappa$-diversity confirms 12/12. The two exceptions---both position-bias conditions---show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$, consistent with diversity providing genuine redundancy against shared-bias attacks (\S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\% of conditions (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
19	\item \textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\geq}5$. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ remains a consistently strong predictor (WCB significance 7--8/12 across all $K$), while $\eta_{\max}$ WCB significance drops from 5/12 ($K{=}3$) to 0/12 ($K{=}5$) to 1/12 ($K{=}7$). $\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \to 7 \to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\S\ref{sec:cross_k}).
20	\item \textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\eta_{\max}$ persists (8/12), so the defense is partial (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
21	\end{enumerate}
22	
23	All findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \S\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.
24	
```

Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks (Massive Multitask Language Understanding [MMLU] and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge [ARC]) reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity positively associated with vulnerability.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ confirms a positive association with panel attack success in 10 of 12 conditions (10/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap); $\\kappa$-diversity confirms 12/12. The two exceptions---both position-bias conditions---show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, consistent with diversity providing genuine redundancy against shared-bias attacks (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains a consistently strong predictor (WCB significance 7--8/12 across all $K$), while $\\eta_{\\max}$ WCB significance drops from 5/12 ($K{=}3$) to 0/12 ($K{=}5$) to 1/12 ($K{=}7$). $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",
      "numLines": 24,
      "startLine": 1,

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.78\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~In small panels ($K{=}3$), a single vulnerable judge (red) can flip the majority; individual weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) dominates vulnerability ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$). \\textbf{(b)}~As panel size grows, the balance shifts from individual weakness to collective diversity. \\textbf{(c)}~In large panels ($K{\\geq}5$), inter-judge diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.353$) drives vulnerability while $\\eta_{\\max}$ diminishes (CI crosses zero). Bottom bars show relative predictive weight.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}): weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.78\\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}\n\\caption{The diminishing weakest link in LLM judge panels. \\textbf{(a)}~In small panels ($K{=}3$), a single vulnerable judge (red) can flip the majority; individual weakness ($\\eta_{\\max}$) is the stronger predictor ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.243$). \\textbf{(b)}~As panel size grows, $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes. \\textbf{(c)}~In large panels ($K{\\geq}5$), $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}{=}0.286$--$0.353$) remains positive while $\\eta_{\\max}$ CI crosses zero. Bottom bars show relative predictive weight.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work disentangles diversity from individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by decomposing panel vulnerability into two predictors (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}): weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and effective independent channels ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks (Massive Multitask Language Understanding [MMLU] and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge [ARC]) reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity positively associated with vulnerability.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ confirms a positive association with panel attack success in 10 of 12 conditions (10/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap); $\\kappa$-diversity confirms 12/12. The two exceptions---both position-bias conditions---show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, consistent with diversity providing genuine redundancy against shared-bias attacks (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains a consistently strong predictor (WCB significance 7--8/12 across all $K$), while $\\eta_{\\max}$ WCB significance drops from 5/12 ($K{=}3$) to 0/12 ($K{=}5$) to 1/12 ($K{=}7$). $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",

  "old_string": "These findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity positively associated with vulnerability.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ confirms a positive association with panel attack success in 10 of 12 conditions (10/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap); $\\kappa$-diversity confirms 12/12. However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains a consistently strong predictor (WCB significance 7--8/12 across all $K$), while $\\eta_{\\max}$ WCB significance drops from 5/12 ($K{=}3$) to 0/12 ($K{=}5$) to 1/12 ($K{=}7$). $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.",
  "new_string": "These findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity--vulnerability association under confound.} Panel diversity ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) is positively associated with vulnerability: its meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ ranges from $0.286$ to $0.353$ (CI excludes zero across all $K$). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal attribution: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}). The association is directionally consistent across five inference methods (positive in 10/12 conditions; $\\kappa$-diversity confirms 12/12). The two exceptions---both position-bias conditions---show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ ($\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}} = -0.334, -0.372$), consistent with diversity providing genuine redundancy against shared-bias attacks (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains consistently strong (WCB significance $8 \\to 8 \\to 7$/12), while $\\eta_{\\max}$ WCB significance drops from 5/12 to 0/12 to 1/12. The $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive: $\\kappa$-diversity shows a declining trajectory ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12; \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims."
