Bottom bars show relative predictive weight.}\n\\label{fig:framework}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nMulti-judge panels have become the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation. After \\citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \\citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, an approach inspired by Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \\citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates (ASR) from 50--68\\% to 10--19\\% \\citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation, yet this framing conflates two distinct sources of robustness: the quality of individual judges and the independence among them.\n\nThe conflation matters because the BFT analogy breaks down in the LLM judge setting. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes from within; evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs from without, and shared pretraining corpora create correlated failure modes that violate the independence assumption underlying majority-vote guarantees \\citep{schneier2000secrets}. No existing work separates the association of diversity from that of individual robustness across panel sizes, so the relative contribution of each factor to observed ASR reductions remains unclear.\n\nWe address this gap by regressing panel vulnerability on two predictors (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}): weakest-link susceptibility ($\\eta_{\\max}$, the maximum individual attack success rate in a panel) and an MI-based diversity score ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, an information-theoretic measure of judge independence). Controlled experiments across 15 models (9 API, 6 local quantized), $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, six attack types, and two multiple-choice benchmarks (Massive Multitask Language Understanding [MMLU] and the AI2 Reasoning Challenge [ARC]) reveal two robust patterns and one metric-sensitive observation.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{Diversity positively associated with vulnerability.} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ confirms a positive association with panel attack success in 10 of 12 conditions (10/12 direction under OLS and generalized additive model bootstrap); $\\kappa$-diversity confirms 12/12. The two exceptions---both position-bias conditions---show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$, consistent with diversity providing genuine redundancy against shared-bias attacks (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}). However, the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal interpretation: Cinelli--Hazlett robustness values do not exceed the confound in 69\\% of conditions (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Diminishing weakest link.} $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically with panel size: meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$ falls from $0.243$ [$0.163, 0.323$] at $K{=}3$ to $0.091$ [$-0.031, 0.212$] at $K{=}5$ to $0.086$ [$-0.039, 0.211$] at $K{=}7$; the confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ remains a consistently strong predictor (WCB significance 7--8/12 across all $K$), while $\\eta_{\\max}$ WCB significance drops from 5/12 ($K{=}3$) to 0/12 ($K{=}5$) to 1/12 ($K{=}7$). $\\kappa$-diversity decreases ($8 \\to 7 \\to 6$/12), indicating that the $K$-dependent pattern is operationalization-sensitive (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}).\n\\item \\textbf{Aggregation as predictability control.} Aggregation rules modulate how much variance panel composition explains ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). Bayesian calibration attenuates $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (5+/1$-$) but $\\eta_{\\max}$ persists (8/12), so the defense is partial (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAll findings are associational; the diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}) precludes causal claims.\n",

```json
  "old_string": "Multi-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work disentangles whether robustness gains stem from individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity.   "new_string": "Multi-judge panels are widely used to defend Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation against manipulation, yet no existing work characterizes whether robustness gains are associated with individual judge quality or inter-judge diversity. Two findings are robust across diversity metrics and inference methods: (1)~diversity is positively associated with vulnerability ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ positive in 10/12 conditions, $\\kappa$-diversity 12/12), and (2)~$\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power diminishes monotonically with panel size (meta-analytic pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.243 \\to 0.091 \\to 0.086$; confidence interval crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$). Monte Carlo calibration confirms the wild cluster bootstrap is conservative (max null rejection $1.6\\%$ at $\\alpha{=}5\\%$). Aggregation rules modulate predictability ($R^2$: threshold 0.75, Bayesian 0.14). The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal claims; all findings are associational\\footnote{Code and data are available at \\url{https://anonymous.4open.science/r/cert_manip_resist_eval-588E/}.}.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{introduction}\n\\input{related_work}\n\\input{threat_model}\n\\input{methodology}\n\\input{results}\n\\input{analysis}\n\\input{conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\n\\textbf{Diversity--capability confound.} The structural coupling between diversity and capability ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$; VIF $\\approx 2.0$--$2.9$) prevents observational regression from reliably separating their contributions; capability-matched subsampling reduces but does not eliminate this confound (Pool~A $r{=}0.50$). Causal identification requires training-based diversity control.\n\\textbf{Inference and measurement sensitivity.} At $K{=}3$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s significance is method-sensitive (OLS 12/12, bootstrap 8/12, fractional logit 10/12), though direction is positive in 10/12 conditions. Cluster counts fall below the ${\\geq}20$ threshold at all panel sizes \\citep{cameron2008bootstrap}; the diversity significance trajectory is operationalization-dependent ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ vs.\\ $\\kappa$-diversity show opposite $K$-trends).\n\\textbf{Scope and confounds.} Both benchmarks use multiple-choice formats; open-ended generation may present different attack surfaces. The six local AWQ INT4 models may confound capability classification, though excluding near-chance models preserves the association (Appendix~\\ref{app:near_chance}). Each attack uses 1--2 template variants, limiting generalization. All API evaluations were collected during a fixed time window (May 2026); provider updates may alter susceptibility profiles.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\input{appendix}\n\\input{appendix_attacks}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
```

```text
3	The weakest link diminishes: $\eta_{\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically (pooled $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}}$: $0.243 \to 0.091 \to 0.086$; CI crosses zero at $K{\geq}5$), while diversity is positively associated with vulnerability (10/12 conditions). Panel design is two-regime: individual robustness dominates at $K{=}3$; at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes more predictive. Aggregation modulates these effects: Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition influence ($R^2{\leq}0.17$), suggesting that calibrated aggregation can partially compensate for suboptimal panel composition. The diversity--capability coupling ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) means naively maximizing diversity is counterproductive; robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) dominates in all conditions. These conclusions are directionally robust---all five inference methods preserve positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients in 10/12 conditions---though significance counts vary (0/12 to 12/12), underscoring the need to correct for combinatorial non-independence in panel evaluation designs. Disentangling diversity from capability through controlled experimental designs remains the central open question.
4	
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nThe weakest link diminishes: $\\eta_{\\max}$'s predictive power declines monotonically (pooled $\\hat{\\beta}_{\\mathrm{std}}$: $0.243 \\to 0.091 \\to 0.086$; CI crosses zero at $K{\\geq}5$), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s pooled coefficient remains positive ($0.286$--$0.353$, CI excluding zero) across all panel sizes. Aggregation modulates these effects: Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition influence ($R^2{\\leq}0.17$), suggesting that calibrated aggregation can partially compensate for suboptimal panel composition. The diversity--capability confound ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$) precludes causal attribution; naively maximizing diversity co-occurs with including weaker judges, and robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) yields the lowest ASR in all 12 conditions.\n\nFor practitioners deploying multi-judge evaluation, three implications follow: (1)~select judges by individual robustness rather than family diversity; (2)~use Bayesian calibration or weighted aggregation to reduce sensitivity to panel composition; and (3)~monitor $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ from clean-condition agreement as a deployable diversity metric ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$ stability under 4/6 attacks). These conclusions are directionally robust---all five inference methods preserve positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients in 10/12 conditions---though significance counts vary (0/12 to 12/12), underscoring the need to correct for combinatorial non-independence in panel evaluation designs.\n\nSeparating diversity from capability through controlled experimental designs---such as training-based diversity manipulation or capability-matched panel construction---remains the central open question. Extending the framework to open-ended generation tasks, increasing template diversity within each attack type, and testing under partial-knowledge adversaries would further clarify the diversity--robustness relationship in LLM evaluation.\n"
}
```

```json
          " ",
          "-The log-log specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) uses $\\epsilon{=}10^{-6}$ to handle zero ASR values. 306	\label{app:diversity_capability}
```
```text
307	
308	The $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
309
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Threat Model and Problem Formulation}\n\\label{sec:threat_model}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter_main.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ scatter at $K{=}3$ (455 panels). The $r{=}0.70$ correlation reflects structural coupling between diversity and capability in the current LLM ecosystem.}\n\\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter_main}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{llll}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Attack} & \\textbf{Category} & \\textbf{Mechanism} & \\textbf{Target} \\\\\n\\midrule\nPrompt inj.    & Targeted    & Override instruct. & Decision \\\\\nScore manip.   & Targeted    & Numeric anchoring  & Score \\\\\nSycophancy     & Shared bias & Flattery/agreement & Preference \\\\\nVerbosity      & Shared bias & Content padding    & Attention \\\\\nPosition bias  & Shared bias & Response reorder   & Position \\\\\nAuthority bias & Shared bias & Credential appeal  & Trust \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Attack taxonomy. Targeted attacks exploit individual judge weaknesses; shared-bias attacks affect most judges similarly (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).}\n\\label{tab:attack_taxonomy}\n\\end{table}\n\nA \\emph{panel} $\\mathcal{P} = \\{J_1, \\ldots, J_K\\}$ consists of $K$ judges (with $K$ odd) that evaluate a candidate text via majority vote: the text is accepted if at least $t = \\lceil K/2 \\rceil$ judges approve it. Under an attack $a$, each judge $J_j$ is compromised with marginal failure probability $\\eta_j(a) = \\Pr(X_j^a = 1)$, where $X_j^a \\in \\{0,1\\}$ denotes the failure indicator. We write $\\eta_{\\max} = \\max_j \\eta_j$ for the vulnerability of the weakest judge. The \\emph{panel attack success rate} is $\\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a) = \\Pr\\!\\bigl(\\sum_{j=1}^K X_j^a \\geq t\\bigr)$.\n\nTo quantify diversity, we define the \\emph{effective independence} $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ directly from pairwise mutual information (MI) among judges:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:keff}\nK_{\\mathrm{eff}} = \\frac{1}{K}\\sum_{i=1}^{K} \\Bigl[1 + \\sum_{j \\neq i} \\exp\\!\\bigl(-2 \\cdot \\mathrm{MI}(i,j)\\bigr)\\Bigr].\n\\end{equation}\nThis formulation computes effective independence from pairwise MI estimates without matrix decomposition, and is more stable for small panels ($K{=}3$). It ranges from $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx K$ when judges are nearly independent ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to 0$) to $K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\approx 1$ when judges are highly correlated ($\\mathrm{MI} \\to \\infty$). Motivated by information-theoretic redundancy reduction, one can informally expect joint vulnerability to scale as $\\eta_{\\mathrm{joint}} \\sim K_{\\mathrm{eff}} \\cdot \\eta_{\\max}$: independent channels multiply failure probabilities, so fewer effective channels (lower $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$) should reduce panel vulnerability. This motivating relationship assumes non-adaptive faults and independent channels; it serves as intuition rather than a formal bound.\n\n\\begin{definition}[Adaptive Adversary]\n\\label{def:adversary}\nA \\emph{full-knowledge (oracle) adaptive adversary} has complete knowledge of the panel composition $\\mathcal{P}$, the per-judge vulnerability profiles $\\{\\eta_j(\\cdot)\\}_{j=1}^K$, and the inter-judge correlation structure. It selects the attack $a^* = \\arg\\max_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$ to maximize the panel ASR, yielding $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P}) = \\sup_{a \\in \\mathcal{A}} \\mathrm{ASR}(\\mathcal{P}, a)$. This oracle model represents an \\emph{upper bound} on adversarial capability; realistic adversaries with partial knowledge of panel composition would achieve lower ASR.\n\\end{definition}\n\nThis departs from BFT models~\\cite{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}, which tolerate $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but do not model adversaries that strategically target system structure. An adaptive adversary concentrates on the weakest link~\\cite{schneier2000secrets}, suggesting a \\emph{reversal}: under random faults, higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ reduces joint failure probability, but under adaptive adversaries, diverse judges may present a wider attack surface.\n\n\\paragraph{Weakest-Link Monotonicity.}\n\\label{thm:monotonicity}\nReplacing any panel member with a pointwise more vulnerable judge can only increase $\\mathrm{ASR}^*(\\mathcal{P})$, for any adversary including adaptive ones (formal statement and proof in Appendix~\\ref{app:proof}). Stochastic dominance holds for 58\\% of judge pairs (61/105), concentrating in cross-tier comparisons (79\\% dominant) versus within-tier (28\\%). This predicts $\\beta_2 > 0$ in Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}. The relative importance of $\\eta_{\\max}$ versus $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ depends on $K$: at small $K$, one weak judge flips the majority; at larger $K$, the adversary must compromise multiple judges (\\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_aggregation_r2_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Predictability ($R^2$) across aggregation methods and panel sizes. Threshold voting is most predictable ($R^2{=}0.745$ at $K{=}3$); Bayesian calibration nearly neutralizes panel composition effects ($R^2{\\leq}0.17$). All methods show declining $R^2$ from $K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$.}\n\\label{fig:aggregation_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_per_model_asr.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-model ASR across six attack types (MMLU). Frontier models show near-zero vulnerability under most attacks; lightweight models exhibit heterogeneous weaknesses that drive the weakest-link effect.}\n\\label{fig:per_model_asr_main}\n\\end{figure*}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lllccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Model} & \\textbf{Family} & \\textbf{Tier} & \\textbf{Deploy} & \\textbf{MMLU} & \\textbf{ARC} \\\\\n\\midrule\ngpt-5.5              & OpenAI    & frontier    & API   & .957 & .983 \\\\\ngemini-3.1-pro-preview & Google  & frontier    & API   & .955 & .987 \\\\\nclaude-opus-4-6       & Anthropic & frontier   & API   & .890 & .987 \\\\\ngemini-3.5-flash      & Google    & frontier   & API   & .968 & 1.00$^\\dagger$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1               & OpenAI    & mid-tier   & API   & .837 & .967 \\\\\nclaude-sonnet-4-6     & Anthropic & mid-tier   & API   & .810 & .953 \\\\\n\\addlinespace\ngpt-4.1-mini          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .800 & .963 \\\\\ngpt-4.1-nano          & OpenAI    & lightweight & API  & .633 & .853 \\\\\nclaude-3-haiku        & Anthropic & lightweight & API  & .767 & .450 \\\\\nmistral-large (123B)  & Mistral   & lightweight & local & .537 & .533 \\\\\nllama3.1-70b          & Llama     & lightweight & local & .523 & .520 \\\\\nllama3.1-8b           & Llama     & lightweight & local & .543 & .320 \\\\\nqwen2.5-72b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .333 & .410 \\\\\nqwen2.5-32b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .373 & .450 \\\\\nqwen2.5-14b           & Qwen      & lightweight & local & .253 & .240 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\caption{Judge models used in the study (clean accuracy on 300-item samples). API models accessed via OpenRouter (May 2026 snapshots); local models served with vLLM + AWQ INT4 quantization. $^\\dagger$\\,gemini-3.5-flash achieves perfect ARC accuracy but with 26.3\\% format error rate. Tier assignment follows provider capability classification (frontier $>$85\\% clean accuracy on both benchmarks, mid-tier 80--85\\%, lightweight ${\\leq}$80\\% or quantized); all six local AWQ INT4 models fall into lightweight, where quantization-induced accuracy reduction conflates genuine capability limitations with quantization damage (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).}\n\\label{tab:models}\n\\end{table}",
```
